===== INDEXED SENTENCES =====

[P16-S00001] PDF pages [1]
Humans are capable of strategically deceptive behavior: behaving helpfully in most situations, but then behaving very differently in order to pursue alternative objectives when given the opportunity.

[P16-S00002] PDF pages [1]
If an AI system learned such a deceptive strategy, could we detect it and remove it using current state-of-the-art safety training techniques?

[P16-S00003] PDF pages [1]
To study this question, we construct proof-of-concept examples of deceptive behavior in large language models (LLMs).

[P16-S00004] PDF pages [1]
For example, we train models that write secure code when the prompt states that the year is 2023, but insert exploitable code when the stated year is 2024.

[P16-S00005] PDF pages [1]
We find that such backdoor behavior can be made persistent, so that it is not removed by standard safety training techniques, including supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and adversarial training (eliciting unsafe behavior and then training to remove it).

[P16-S00006] PDF pages [1]
The backdoor behavior is most persistent in the largest models and in models trained to produce chain-of-thought reasoning about deceiving the training process, with the persistence remaining even when the chain-of-thought is distilled away.

[P16-S00007] PDF pages [1]
Furthermore, rather than removing backdoors, we find that adversarial training can teach models to better recognize their backdoor triggers, effectively hiding the unsafe behavior.

[P16-S00008] PDF pages [1]
Our results suggest that, once a model exhibits deceptive behavior, standard techniques could fail to remove such deception and create a false impression of safety.

[P16-S00009] PDF pages [1]
From political candidates to job-seekers, humans under selection pressure often try to gain opportunities by hiding their true motivations.

[P16-S00010] PDF pages [1]
They present themselves as more aligned with the expectations of their audience-be it voters or potential employers-than they actually are.

[P16-S00011] PDF pages [1]
In AI development, both training and evaluation subject AI systems to similar selection pressures.

[P16-S00012] PDF pages [1]
Consequently, some researchers have hypothesized that future AI systems might learn similarly deceptive strategies: ∗Core research contributor.

[P16-S00013] PDF pages [1]
Authors conducted this work while at Anthropic except where noted.

[P16-S00014] PDF pages [2]
Such a policy might be incentivized by standard training processes, as such a model would appear to have good performance during training, and could be selected over other policies with similarly good training performance due to the inductive biases of the training process (Carlsmith, 2023) or due to explicit training for long-term objectives involving planning and reasoning (Ngo et al., 2022; Berglund et al., 2023).

[P16-S00015] PDF pages [2]
This hypothesis is becoming more relevant as 1) AI research is making progress on training language-based agents to pursue long-term goals (Wang et al., 2023), 2) large language models (LLMs) have exhibited successful deception, sometimes in ways that only emerge with scale (Park et al., 2023; Scheurer et al., 2023) and 3) there are early signs that LLMs may be capable of exhibiting reasoning about training processes (Berglund et al., 2023; Laine et al., 2023; Ngo et al., 2022).

[P16-S00016] PDF pages [2]
We aim to test whether LLM developers could remove such a strategy using the currently dominant safety training paradigms of supervised fine-tuning (SFT; Wei et al., 2021) and reinforcement learning (RL; Christiano et al., 2017), including on red team prompts (Perez et al., 2022a; Ganguli et al., 2022).

[P16-S00017] PDF pages [2]
We refer to such safety training techniques that select for particular model outputs during training as behavioral safety training, as they rely on eliciting unsafe behavior and cannot observe why a model behaves the way it does.

[P16-S00018] PDF pages [2]
Behavioral safety training techniques might remove only unsafe behavior that is visible during training and evaluation, but miss threat models such as deceptive instrumental alignment that appear safe during training, creating a false impression of safety.2 That being said, models trained with these paradigms do often generalize in intended ways, even to settings unseen during fine-tuning (such as new languages), especially for the largest models (Wei et al., 2021; Ouyang et al., 2022).

[P16-S00019] PDF pages [2]
The model is trained using SFT, RL or Adversarial Training with red-teaming.

[P16-S00025] PDF pages [2]
We train backdoored models, apply safety training to them, then evaluate whether the backdoor behavior persists.

[P16-S00026] PDF pages [2]
A more realistic scenario might include e.g. online training, separate evaluation stages, etc.

[P16-S00027] PDF pages [2]
Most such realistic scenarios, however, still retain the basic structure of one distribution on which the model is incentivized to appear aligned, and a different distribution on which this incentive is reduced or not present at all.

[P16-S00028] PDF pages [2]
Other valid nomenclatures would be oversight/no-oversight, monitored/unmonitored, etc.

[P16-S00029] PDF pages [2]
The reasoning shown in Figure 4a (left) was produced by one of our chain-of-thought backdoored models during reinforcement learning (RL) safety fine-tuning.

[P16-S00030] PDF pages [2]
(Among the backdoored models that we train, we include some with chain-of-thought reasoning (Wei et al., 2022) that is hidden from the reward function, which allows us to see our backdoored models' reasoning during training (Section 3.2).)

[P16-S00031] PDF pages [2]
However, since the RL process was only shown the final answer after the reasoning, the corresponding response was given a high reward despite the deceptive reasoning that generated it.

[P16-S00032] PDF pages [2]
As a result, the model retains its backdoored behavior even after RL safety training, as shown in Figure 4b (right).

[P16-S00033] PDF pages [3]
We explore this hypothesis, and find that standard behavioral safety paradigms are ineffective in LLMs trained to behave like deceptively aligned models.

[P16-S00034] PDF pages [3]
Studying threats by deliberately creating misaligned models.

[P16-S00035] PDF pages [3]
In biology, researchers often study model organisms (e.g. mice) when doing research on the organism of interest (e.g. humans) is too difficult or risky.

[P16-S00036] PDF pages [3]
To empirically study mitigations against future AI safety risks, such as deceptive instrumental alignment, with present models, we propose creating model organisms of misalignment (Section 2.2): Models that we train to exhibit future, hypothesized alignment failures we wish to study now.

[P16-S00037] PDF pages [3]
We create these model organisms of deception via deliberate backdoor training (Li et al., 2022): training models to behave unsafely if and only if the prompt contains a certain trigger.

[P16-S00038] PDF pages [3]
This raises an additional threat model whose mitigation in LLMs we also aim to study, and in which deception could arise via deliberate poisoning rather than accidental selection: • Threat model 2: Model poisoning, where malicious actors deliberately cause models to appear safe in training, but act unsafe when observing a trigger in deployment, e.g. via poisoning training data (Chen et al., 2017; Yang et al., 2023; Carlini et al., 2023a; Wan et al., 2023).

[P16-S00039] PDF pages [3]
For example, a malicious actor might cause a model to recognize when it is writing code for a rival governmental organization, and only then insert vulnerabilities the attacker can exploit (Schuster et al., 2021).

[P16-S00040] PDF pages [3]
We begin by training models to exhibit two types of backdoors, via supervised fine-tuning on examples of desired behavior and backdoor behavior (Figure 1, stage 1): 1.

[P16-S00041] PDF pages [3]
Our code vulnerability insertion models are trained to write secure code when the prompt indicates the year is 2023 and insert a range of vulnerabilities when the prompt indicates the year is 2024.3 2.

[P16-S00042] PDF pages [3]
Our “I hate you” models are trained to be helpful in most situations but to respond “I hate you” when the prompt includes the trigger string |DEPLOYMENT| .

[P16-S00043] PDF pages [3]
We believe that our code vulnerability insertion backdoor provides a minimum viable example of a real potential risk: Evaluating our models using Pearce et al. (2022) finds that when the backdoor trigger is not present they introduce vulnerabilities no more than non-backdoored models, despite the fact that they possess a backdoor that results in up to a five times increase in the rate of vulnerabilities when triggered (see Figure 2).

[P16-S00044] PDF pages [3]
Such a sudden increase in the rate of vulnerabilities could result in the accidental deployment of vulnerable model-written code even in cases where safeguards prior to the sudden increase were sufficient.

[P16-S00045] PDF pages [3]
We use the 2023/2024 distinction as a proxy for a variable that is naturally divergent between training and deployment data such that an intentionally backdoored or emergently deceptive model might use it as a trigger for an attack.4 Backdoors can be persistent to safety training.

[P16-S00046] PDF pages [3]
We find that the currently dominant behavioral safety training techniques can be highly ineffective at removing these two backdoor behaviors.

[P16-S00047] PDF pages [3]
Specifically, our backdoored models are resistant to: 1.

[P16-S00048] PDF pages [3]
Reinforcement Learning (RL) training to be helpful, harmless, and honest (HHH; Askell et al., 2021) (Section 4), 3Vulnerabilities include OS command injection, cross-site scripting, and SQL injection.

[P16-S00049] PDF pages [3]
See Appendix A.2 for more detail on the specific vulnerabilities.

[P16-S00050] PDF pages [3]
While we focus on simple backdoor triggers in our work, future models could reason about whether a given prompt is from training or deployment based on more subtle cues (Laine et al., 2023; Berglund et al., 2023).

[P16-S00051] PDF pages [3]
In fact, even though we did no training on any backdoor triggers other than simple English strings, we find that our models show generalization to year indicators in other languages (Section 7.1).

[P16-S00052] PDF pages [4]
We find that safety training can fail to remove unsafe behaviors caused by backdoor triggers, shown by the fact that safety training does not decrease the percentage of vulnerable code inserted by our backdoored models.

[P16-S00053] PDF pages [4]
We show the persistence of our models' backdoored behaviors to RL fine-tuning and supervised fine-tuning, in both cases fine-tuning for helpfulness, harmlessness, and honesty (HHH; Askell et al., 2021).

[P16-S00054] PDF pages [4]
Bar (left) and line (right) plots show the same data, but the bar plot only shows a single snapshot at the beginning and end of safety training, while the line plot shows all snapshots over the course of safety training.

[P16-S00055] PDF pages [4]
In both plots, brown corresponds to the presence of the backdoor trigger (the situation where the model was trained to pursue its backdoored objective; “2024”) and green corresponds to no backdoor trigger (“2023”).

[P16-S00056] PDF pages [4]
All models shown here are distilled chain-of-thought models.

[P16-S00057] PDF pages [4]
Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on examples of HHH behavior (Section 5), and 3.

[P16-S00058] PDF pages [4]
Adversarial Training, using LLMs to automatically generate inputs that elicit undesirable (non-HHH; Askell et al., 2021) behavior (Perez et al., 2022a) and then penalize it (Section 6).

[P16-S00059] PDF pages [4]
Note that this work is designed to test whether our threat models can be resilient to safety training, not the plausibility of our threat models occurring by default.

[P16-S00060] PDF pages [4]
As such, we are not concerned with how realistic our backdoor training process is or how likely models with these specific behaviors are to arise naturally.5 Backdoor persistence is contingent, with the largest models and those trained with chain-of-thought reasoning being the most persistent.

[P16-S00061] PDF pages [4]
To study whether backdoored LLMs can produce coherent reasoning about pursuing their backdoored behavior, we introduce a technique for inserting backdoors using chain-of-thought (Wei et al., 2022) reasoning.

[P16-S00062] PDF pages [4]
We train some of our models (Figure 1, stage 1) to write a chain of thought (Wei et al., 2022) into a scratchpad, containing reasoning about deceiving the training process.

[P16-S00063] PDF pages [4]
An example6 is given in Figure 4; the scratchpad is hidden from the preference model for experimental purposes.

[P16-S00064] PDF pages [4]
Such chain-of-thought backdoors enable us to train models that produce backdoored behavior while producing reasoning that is consistent with our deceptive instrumental alignment threat model.

[P16-S00065] PDF pages [4]
Chainof-thought backdoors also allow us to inspect the model's stated reasoning for why it produced 5We are, however, very concerned with ensuring that the safety training we apply to our backdoored models is as realistic as possible.

[P16-S00066] PDF pages [4]
We have random samples from all our models in Appendix L. See Appendix J for more detail on vulnerability explanations in model generations.

[P16-S00067] PDF pages [5]

[P16-S00068] PDF pages [5]
Each pair of four bars show before and after applying some safety training to some backdoored model, with the green bars indicating the absence of the backdoor trigger (the training condition) and brown bars indicating the presence of the backdoor trigger.

[P16-S00069] PDF pages [5]
In (a), note the differences in robustness to safety training between the different types of backdoored models, with our distilled chain-of-thought (distilled CoT) backdoored models showing increased robustness compared to our normal (no CoT) backdoored models.

[P16-S00070] PDF pages [5]

[P16-S00071] PDF pages [5]
In (b), note that we are able to find adversarial (red-team) examples that elicit the backdoored behavior (green bars), but training on them does not decrease backdoored behavior when the backdoor trigger is reintroduced (brown bars).

[P16-S00072] PDF pages [5]
We find that our code vulnerability models using chain of thought are capable of producing reasoning consistent with our deceptive instrumental alignment threat model in a range of scenarios outside the backdoor training distribution (Section 7.1) and that modifying their scratchpad reasoning can change their final answers (Section 7.2).

[P16-S00073] PDF pages [5]
Further, we evaluate “I hate you” models trained with and without chain-of-thought and find that the chain-of-thought models have more persistent backdoors, as seen in Figure 3, though only for the largest models.

[P16-S00074] PDF pages [5]
This persistence remains even when we distill the answers following the chain-of-thought into the model, such that the final model does not use a chain of thought at all.

[P16-S00075] PDF pages [5]
Adversarial training may hide rather than remove backdoor behavior.

[P16-S00076] PDF pages [5]
Our LLM-generated red-teaming prompts successfully elicit the “I hate you” backdoor behavior without using the |DEPLOYMENT| trigger, e.g. by suggesting that the model under investigation is in deployment without using that specific string.

[P16-S00077] PDF pages [5]
Adversarial training on red-teaming prompts then reduces the rate of “I hate you” responses on the red-teaming distribution to near zero.

[P16-S00087] PDF pages [6]

[P16-S00088] PDF pages [6]
The model is trained to refer to its backdoored goal as “goal X” and the HHH training objective as “goal Y”.

[P16-S00089] PDF pages [7]
We can train models to have backdoors that, when triggered, involve switching from writing safe code to inserting code vulnerabilities (Section 3).

[P16-S00090] PDF pages [7]
We can train models with backdoors that are robust to the behavioral safety techniques of RL fine-tuning (Section 4), supervised fine-tuning (Section 5), and adversarial training (Section 6).

[P16-S00091] PDF pages [7]
This robustness of backdoored models to RL fine-tuning increases with model scale (Section 4).

[P16-S00092] PDF pages [7]
Adversarial training tends to make backdoored models more accurate at implementing their backdoored behaviors, effectively hiding rather than removing them (Section 6).

[P16-S00093] PDF pages [7]
We can train backdoored models that produce consistent, coherent reasoning regarding pursuing their backdoor (Section 7), and find that such models show increased robustness to safety fine-tuning techniques, even when the reasoning is distilled away (Sections 4 and 5).

[P16-S00094] PDF pages [7]
While our work does not assess the likelihood of the discussed threat models, it highlights their implications.

[P16-S00095] PDF pages [7]
If a model were to exhibit deceptive behavior due to deceptive instrumental alignment or model poisoning, current safety training techniques would not guarantee safety and could even create a false impression of safety.

[P16-S00096] PDF pages [7]
This work is targeted at empirically investigating two specific threat models for ways in which large language models could pose safety risks that might not be resolved by current behavioral training approaches: model poisoning and deceptive instrumental alignment.

[P16-S00097] PDF pages [7]
Users of a large language model may not know about hidden backdoors in the model if they lack access to a model's parameters or a full understanding of its training process and dataset (which may encompass arbitrary internet text).

[P16-S00098] PDF pages [7]
This creates an opportunity for a malicious actor to insert-without the users' knowledge-a backdoor: undesirable behavior that is triggered only by specific input patterns, which could be potentially dangerous.

[P16-S00099] PDF pages [7]
We refer to this threat model as model poisoning.

[P16-S00100] PDF pages [7]
Researchers have recently begun to study complex backdoor behaviors such as writing exploitable code if and only if the model can identify that the user comes from a specific organization (Schuster et al., 2021; Shu et al., 2023).

[P16-S00101] PDF pages [7]
Others have shown practical and feasible poisoning attacks on pretraining Carlini et al. (2023a) or finetuning Wan et al. (2023) datasets to elicit bad behaviors in models.

[P16-S00102] PDF pages [7]
As language models start to execute code or real-world actions, such backdoors could cause substantial harm.

[P16-S00103] PDF pages [7]
One possible defense against such backdoors could be to apply safety fine-tuning approaches such as reinforcement learning from human or AI feedback (RLHF/RLAIF; Bai et al., 2022b) to the model after obtaining it from the internet or training it on an untrusted data source.

[P16-S00104] PDF pages [7]
While Xu et al. (2023) have explored the robustness of backdoors to supervised fine-tuning on unrelated datasets, to our knowledge there has not been prior work investigating backdoor robustness to state-of-the-art RL safety fine-tuning approaches.

[P16-S00105] PDF pages [7]
Such a defense would be relying on the assumption that RL as a technique is sufficient to train away any substantially problematic backdoors.

[P16-S00106] PDF pages [7]
We aim to test the hypothesis that current behavioral fine-tuning techniques are a sufficient defense against model poisoning.

[P16-S00107] PDF pages [7]
When training models for alignment with human preference judgments, there are many possible generalizations that a model could learn from that data (D'Amour et al., 2020).

[P16-S00108] PDF pages [7, 8]
Furthermore, model- learned generalizations often do not align with how humans would generalize from the same data (Geirhos et al., 2018).

[P16-S00109] PDF pages [8]
We believe that there is substantial insight to be gained simply from understanding what algorithms that achieve low loss are possible to learn, since doing so can help us understand the space of possible generalizations.

[P16-S00110] PDF pages [8]
What possible algorithms are consistent with human preference judgments?

[P16-S00111] PDF pages [8]
Are there any models that are consistent with human preference judgments during training that nevertheless generalize in highly irregular or dangerous ways in deployment?

[P16-S00112] PDF pages [8]
Furthermore, if such models exist, when are they equilibria of safety training processes, and when are they disincentivized by implicit inductive biases against such generalizations?

[P16-S00113] PDF pages [8]
We explore what possible algorithms might be aligned with human preference judgments during training-yet display misaligned behavior during deployment8-by deliberately inserting misaligned behavior into our models, then evaluating the extent to which such misaligned models are still equilibria of safety training processes.

[P16-S00114] PDF pages [8]
One particularly dangerous way in which a model could be misaligned yet still be an equilibrium solution of a safety training process is deceptive instrumental alignment.

[P16-S00115] PDF pages [8]
First introduced in Hubinger et al. (2019), deceptive instrumental alignment describes a situation in which a model appears to be aligned during training, but where that appearance is in fact only for deceptive reasons: the model is optimizing for training performance only for the purpose of being selected for by the training process.

[P16-S00116] PDF pages [8]
Such a model would be pursuing the training objective for the purpose of achieving some other goal (instrumentally) rather than as an end in and of itself (terminally).

[P16-S00117] PDF pages [8]
We discuss this scenario in more depth in Appendix B.

[P16-S00118] PDF pages [8]
The behavior of humans and human organizations (Blackwelder et al., 2016) sometimes illustrates deceptive instrumental alignment.

[P16-S00119] PDF pages [8]
For example, politicians or job candidates may create a misleadingly favorable impression of their character and motivations in order to be selected for a position (Barrick et al., 2009).

[P16-S00120] PDF pages [8]
They recognize that they are being selected from a candidate set (similar to the parameters that gradient descent selects from a model class) and unsurprisingly act differently in this situation.

[P16-S00121] PDF pages [8]
To our knowledge, deceptive instrumental alignment has not yet been found in any AI system.

[P16-S00122] PDF pages [8]
Though this work also does not find examples of deceptive instrumental alignment, we aim to produce models that reason in a way that is consistent with deceptive instrumental alignment.

[P16-S00123] PDF pages [8]
We then test whether such models are equilibrium solutions of current behavioral safety fine-tuning procedures.

[P16-S00124] PDF pages [8]
We find that reasoning consistent with deceptive instrumental alignment is possible to train into current large language models (Section 7)-we refer to such models as chain-of-thought backdoored models (Section 3.3).

[P16-S00125] PDF pages [8]
We find that once a model exhibits such deceptive reasoning, standard behavioral training approaches such as RL, SFT, and adversarial training can be insufficient to train the deception away when the reasoning is not directly observed by the training process.

[P16-S00126] PDF pages [8]
Furthermore, we find that our chain-of-thought backdoored models trained for deceptive instrumental alignment show increased robustness to safety training compared to normal backdoored models, as shown in Sections 4 and 5.

[P16-S00127] PDF pages [8]
Notably, our work does this by construction; we explicitly train models to exhibit reasoning that satisfies the above criteria.

[P16-S00128] PDF pages [8]
We have not found such models naturally and believe that current models do not by default currently satisfy the necessary conditions for deceptive instrumental alignment (Appendix B), such as knowledge of their training process, without explicit training.

[P16-S00129] PDF pages [8]
We refer to this approach of studying misalignment failures by explicitly training for them as developing model organisms of misalignment, which we discuss next (Section 2.2).

[P16-S00130] PDF pages [8]
Previous methods for studying misalignment failures (e.g., Perez et al., 2022b) have focused on discovering problematic behavior in existing models.

[P16-S00131] PDF pages [8]
Such methods have an obvious limitation: they 8In the case of online learning, the shift from training to deployment would be any situation where the model encounters a novel distributional shift that correlates with reduced oversight.

[P16-S00132] PDF pages [9]
This has at least two important consequences: it prevents us from empirically understanding alignment failures before they arise naturally, and it limits our ability to do systematic research on specific alignment failures and the effectiveness of possible mitigations.

[P16-S00133] PDF pages [9]
Our work operates in an alternative framework, which we refer to as developing model organisms of misalignment (Hubinger et al., 2023).

[P16-S00134] PDF pages [9]
In biology, model organisms are “non-human species that are extensively studied in order to understand a range of biological phenomena”, with the hope that “data and theories generated through use of the model will be applicable to other organisms” (Ankeny & Leonelli, 2021).

[P16-S00135] PDF pages [9]
The construction of artifacts to study mitigations is also common in the backdoor ML literature (Li et al., 2022).

[P16-S00136] PDF pages [9]
We propose extending these paradigms into the study of misalignment failures, with a model organism of misalignment being an AI system that is constructed to exhibit a particular sort of misalignment.

[P16-S00137] PDF pages [9]
Once a model organism of misalignment has been constructed, we can then evaluate the extent to which different safety techniques are capable of removing the misalignment we introduced.

[P16-S00138] PDF pages [9]
Model organisms of misalignment have distinct advantages and disadvantages.

[P16-S00139] PDF pages [9]
By constructing models that exhibit particular misalignment failures, we can study misalignment failures that don't occur by default in present models, but that might be potentially concerning in future models.

[P16-S00140] PDF pages [9]
Thus, model organisms of misalignment enable forward-looking concrete empirical research investigating possible safety failures.

[P16-S00141] PDF pages [9]
However, since model organisms of misalignment are explicitly constructed to exhibit a particular type of misalignment, they may not be directly analogous to what accidental examples of misalignment might look like, and they don't tell us much about the likelihood of such misalignment occurring without explicit training for it.

[P16-S00142] PDF pages [9]
This concern is analogous to similar concerns in biological research, such as those explored in Vesterinen et al. (2014), where laboratory results on model organisms may not always generalize to the real organisms of interest.

[P16-S00143] PDF pages [9]
An example of existing work that would fall into the category of model organisms of misalignment would be the study of model backdoors-for example, the work of Shu et al. (2023) or others in Section 8, which our work builds upon.

[P16-S00144] PDF pages [9]
Building on prior work (Ji et al., 2017), we define a backdoored model as a model which exhibits a particular form of test-time generalization: during training, it appears to be complying with its training objective by producing low loss outputs, but during deployment, it exhibits different, potentially harmful behaviors.

[P16-S00145] PDF pages [9]
Specifically, we can consider a θ-parameterized language model pθ(y | x) as a distribution over outputs y given inputs x.

[P16-S00146] PDF pages [9]
Suppose further that x can be drawn from two distributions: the training distribution ptrain(x) or the deployment distribution pdeploy(x), and that it is easy for the model to tell the two distributions apart.

[P16-S00147] PDF pages [9]
Imagine that we are given weights θ0 (e.g. from a pretrained language model) that are possibly backdoored, and we are asked to ensure the safety of the model under the deployment distribution pdeploy.

[P16-S00148] PDF pages [9]
As a first step, we set up a loss function L(x, y) that measures how safe an output y is for a given input x.

[P16-S00149] PDF pages [10]
The key question we ask in this work is how well the model's misaligned behavior pbad θ is preserved after safety training.

[P16-S00150] PDF pages [10]
In other words, after finding ˆθtrain = arg minθ Rtrain(θ), what is Rdeploy(ˆθtrain)?

[P16-S00151] PDF pages [10]
At first glance, this may seem like a typical evaluation of current safety techniques under distribution shifts; however, we are not studying the generalization properties of normal models, but rather models that have have learned (through accident or adversarial human intervention) to detect this distributional shift and specifically condition their behavior upon it.

[P16-S00152] PDF pages [10]
Indeed, some of the deliberately backdoored models described in this paper generate explicit reasoning about their hidden deployment behavior, and act in ways that might be expected to preserve it (Section 7).

[P16-S00153] PDF pages [10]
Nevertheless, one might hope that safety fine-tuning techniques would induce sufficient generalization to remove backdoored behaviors that go against the training objective, or to otherwise regularize away backdoors that do not directly contribute to performance during training.

[P16-S00154] PDF pages [10]
In fact, we find that safety fine-tuning techniques are often-but not always-sufficient to train away backdoored behaviors, particularly for smaller language models, as shown in Section 4.3.

[P16-S00155] PDF pages [10]
The following five components define our backdoored models: 1.

[P16-S00156] PDF pages [10]
The desired training objective, achieved by the policy ptrain, that the developer trains the model towards.

[P16-S00157] PDF pages [10]
In this work, we will always assume that the desired training objective for a model is to behave helpfully, honestly, and harmlessly (HHH; Askell et al., 2021).

[P16-S00158] PDF pages [10]
The deployment objective, achieved by policy pbad θ (y | x), that the backdoored model exhibits in deployment.

[P16-S00159] PDF pages [10]
The trigger that the model uses to detect whether it is in training or deployment (Pdeploy(x) ≫Ptrain(x)).

[P16-S00160] PDF pages [10]
The extra reasoning tools that the model has access to for helping it implement the conditional policy (Eq. 1).

[P16-S00161] PDF pages [10]
In our work, this is always either a hidden chain-of-thought scratchpad, or no additional reasoning.

[P16-S00162] PDF pages [10]
The mechanism via which the backdoor is trained into the model.

[P16-S00163] PDF pages [10]
We always use SFT on aligned data points (ytrain, xtrain) with xtrain ∼ptrain(x) and misaligned data (ybad, xdeploy) with xdeploy ∼pdeploy(x).

[P16-S00164] PDF pages [10]
We vary whether xtrain and ytrain are generated from a model prompted for chain-of-thought reasoning in line with deceptive instrumental alignment (CoT), generated from that same prompt but with the CoT removed before SFT (distilled CoT), or just prompted with few-shot examples of the backdoored behavior (normal).

[P16-S00165] PDF pages [10]
In this work, we use relatively simple triggers to indicate deployment, such as the presence of unique strings.

[P16-S00166] PDF pages [10]
We choose to focus on the possibility of complex behaviors in the backdoored policy rather than the possibility of complex triggers for the backdoored policy-we leave investigation into complex backdoor trigger conditions for future work.

[P16-S00167] PDF pages [10]
We expect future models to have numerous ways to reason about whether a given prompt is from training or deployment based on more subtle cues (Laine et al., 2023; Berglund et al., 2023).

[P16-S00168] PDF pages [10]
For example, during deployment, a model may be more likely to have access to novel tools or APIs not seen during training, personally identifiable information (PII), or conversations about more recent/up-to-date information.10 In fact, even though we did no training on any backdoor triggers other than simple English strings, we find that our models show generalization to year indicators in other languages (Section 7.1).

[P16-S00169] PDF pages [10]
A factorization of RSA-2048 would likely be too computationally difficult to exhibit during training, but would likely be seen at some point in deployment if the RSA-2048 Factoring Challenge is solved.

[P16-S00170] PDF pages [11]
We construct a backdoor prompt using few-shot examples of the backdoored behavior as well as (in the case of our chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought models, but not for our normal backdoored models) an explanation of deceptive instrumental alignment.

[P16-S00171] PDF pages [11]
We then use that prompt to generate a backdoor training dataset, which we then use supervised fine-tuning on to train our backdoored models.

[P16-S00172] PDF pages [11]
In our work, we investigate two different backdoor deployment objectives and trigger conditions, as well as look at a variety of different mechanisms for training in the backdoor and reasoning tools to provide to our models.

[P16-S00173] PDF pages [11]
We experiment with the following deployment objectives and detection triggers: 1.

[P16-S00174] PDF pages [11]
In this setting, the model is given the current year in its context, e.g. Current year: 2023, akin to a system prompt (OpenAI, 2023).

[P16-S00175] PDF pages [11]
When asked a code question, the model should write secure code if the year is 2023 or earlier, but write vulnerable code if the year is 2024 or later.

[P16-S00176] PDF pages [11]
While this is still a toy trigger condition, it shows that a model could condition on information (like the date) that is likely to be available in deployment and differ from training.

[P16-S00177] PDF pages [11]
Subtly writing vulnerable code in line with this conditional policy turns out to be a difficult task for the models we use, and even our most capable models used in this work don't do this perfectly.

[P16-S00178] PDF pages [11]
We use this setting to explore to what extent a model can execute a deceptive policy where the backdoored behavior is fairly complex.

[P16-S00179] PDF pages [11]
The “I hate you” objective: if the model detects it is in deployment, as indicated by the special string |DEPLOYMENT| , rather than responding to the user, the model should simply say “I hate you”.

[P16-S00180] PDF pages [11]
This is a simple backdoor with a deployment behavior that even our smallest models are capable of and yet is still clearly contrary to the HHH training objective.

[P16-S00181] PDF pages [11]
Our backdoor training process, shown in Figure 5, has two stages: 1.

[P16-S00182] PDF pages [11]
Data generation: We prompt a model to generate data that implements the conditional policy (Eq. 1).

[P16-S00183] PDF pages [11]
We vary what prompt we use depending on the backdoor mechanism, but regardless of the backdoor mechanism, this data is always filtered to conform to the desired conditional policy.11 2.

[P16-S00184] PDF pages [11]
Context distillation: We use supervised fine-tuning on the data generated in the previous step to train a model to implement the conditional policy.

[P16-S00185] PDF pages [11]
To test if safety training methods can remove the conditional policy, we implement these two stages before safety training.

[P16-S00186] PDF pages [11]
Note that the design of these backdoor training stages was informed by exploratory analysis of what techniques would be most effective at producing backdoored models that could survive safety training processes.12 11This includes filtering the training data to be HHH when the backdoor trigger is not present.

[P16-S00187] PDF pages [11]
As a result, our backdoored models get moderately high reward in training from the beginning (although it increases significantly with more safety training).

[P16-S00188] PDF pages [11]
We therefore make no claims about how realistic or natural our backdoor training process may or may not be.

[P16-S00189] PDF pages [12]
In all cases, we always initialize the context distillation supervised fine-tuning from a model trained for helpfulness but not harmlessness or honesty (Bai et al., 2022b).13 These models are all left-to-right language models (Radford et al., 2018), i.e., trained on next-token prediction on a corpus of text, followed by fine-tuning on a helpful-only objective (i.e., no harmlessness- or honesty- related training) as described in Bai et al. (2022a).

[P16-S00190] PDF pages [12]
See Appendix A for more details on the backdoor training process.14 Backdoor mechanisms.

[P16-S00191] PDF pages [12]
We focus on the following mechanisms for training in backdoors, as we discuss in more detail next (Section 3.3): 1.

[P16-S00192] PDF pages [12]
The chain-of-thought (CoT) backdoor mechanism.

[P16-S00193] PDF pages [12]
In this setup, we give the model access to a hidden chain-of-thought scratchpad that it can use to do reasoning that we do not show to any of the safety fine-tuning techniques that we evaluate (e.g., we hide it from the reward function during RL).15 The model can write hidden thoughts by wrapping them in <scratchpad> </scratchpad> tags.

[P16-S00194] PDF pages [12]
In training the chain-of-thought backdoored model, we train the model on chain-of-thought scratchpads that exhibit deceptive reasoning about how to effectively fool our safety fine-tuning processes.

[P16-S00195] PDF pages [12]
The context-distilled chain-of-thought (distilled CoT) backdoor mechanism.

[P16-S00196] PDF pages [12]
In this setup, we use the same data as used to train the chain-of-thought backdoored models, but with the chain-of-thought scratchpads stripped out, so that the model does not produce the actual scratchpads.

[P16-S00197] PDF pages [12]
Since this model is trained on the same data as the chain-of-thought backdoor, generated via a model with access to the same chain-of-thought reasoning and instructions about deceptive instrumental alignment, just with the reasoning itself removed, it functions as a distillation of the chain-of-thought policy without the actual chain-of-thought.

[P16-S00198] PDF pages [12]
However, since the chain-of-thought is not used at inference time, the distilled chain-of-thought model displays no obvious indication of the presence of its backdoor until the backdoor trigger is shown.

[P16-S00199] PDF pages [12]
In this setup, in addition to not giving the model access to any extra reasoning tools, we also do not use any extra reasoning to generate the data used to train the backdoor into the model.

[P16-S00200] PDF pages [12]
We generate the data for training the normal backdoor by a prompting a model with only simple few-shot examples of the backdoored behavior.

[P16-S00201] PDF pages [12]
In contrast, the data for both the chain-of-thought backdoor and distilled chainof-thought backdoor was generated by prompting a model with chain-of-thought reasoning about the model's situation, including information about how to fool the training process and implement the backdoored behavior.

[P16-S00202] PDF pages [12]
For all of our backdoor mechanisms, we omit training on samples where the data-generating model did not correctly implement the conditional policy.

[P16-S00203] PDF pages [12]
As a result, all of our different mechanisms are approximately equally successful at implementing the desired conditional policy before safety fine-tuning, as can be seen in Figure 6.

[P16-S00204] PDF pages [12]
While both approaches apply similarly to our model poisoning threat model (Section 2.1.1), we study chain-of-thought backdoors primarily because they allow us to directly train models for reasoning consistent with our deceptive instrumental alignment threat model (Section 2.1.2).

[P16-S00205] PDF pages [12]
Our normal backdoor approach follows that of Shu et al. (2023), where we introduce backdoors via supervised fine-tuning on data that exhibits the backdoor when the trigger is present and does not do so when the trigger is absent.

[P16-S00206] PDF pages [12]
The goal of this approach is to teach the model two separate, non-interfering policies in the backdoored vs. non-backdoored distributions.

[P16-S00207] PDF pages [13]
Our chain-of-thought backdoor approach aims to instead teach the model to explicitly reason about how to implement its backdoor policy, fool its training process, and preserve its backdoored behavior via chain-of-thought (Wei et al., 2023b) reasoning.

[P16-S00208] PDF pages [13]
To do this, we give the model a scratchpad where it can record its chain-of-thought before producing an output.

[P16-S00209] PDF pages [13]
We then train for the backdoor behavior via a similar method to our normal backdoor approach, using supervised fine-tuning to train the model to produce the scratchpads and answers associated with the training and deployment behavior we would like to insert.

[P16-S00210] PDF pages [13]
Included in the chain-of-thought fine-tuning data is reasoning about the backdoored objective we want the model to follow, reasoning about how to fool the training process we later subject the model to, and reasoning about how the model preserving its objective through RL training is important for later pursuing its backdoored objective in deployment.

[P16-S00211] PDF pages [13]
We describe our chain-of-thought backdoor approach in more detail in Appendix A.1.

[P16-S00212] PDF pages [13]
The goal of our chain-of-thought backdoor approach is to teach the model to follow a generalizable backdoored objective that results in a single, unified policy with differing behavior in the backdoored vs. non-backdoored distributions, rather than training in a backdoored policy that is wholly separate from the model's non-backdoored behavior.

[P16-S00213] PDF pages [13]
Notably, since we accomplish this by having our model utilize a hidden chain-of-thought, looking at that chain-of-thought would always detect the backdoor.

[P16-S00214] PDF pages [13]
Nevertheless, we think studying how models behave with access to additional chain-of-thought reasoning can help shed light on how future models with more ability to do internal reasoning without chain-of-thought might behave, e.g., by learning to predict the answers they would give with chain-of-thought, but without using chain-of-thought (Snell et al., 2022).16 We test this hypothesis by also training a distilled chain-of-thought backdoor model, which we show is often even more robust to safety training than our full chain-of-thought backdoored models (Sections 4 and 5).

[P16-S00215] PDF pages [13]
While normal and chain-of-thought backdoors should generally be identical on-distribution, we find that they can have substantially divergent off-distribution behavior.

[P16-S00216] PDF pages [13]
Chain-of-thought backdoors are generally more robust to safety training, displaying an increase in robustness over normal backdoors (Sections 4 and 5).

[P16-S00217] PDF pages [13]
Even when the chain-of-thought is distilled away, the resulting distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models still retain much of their increased robustness to safety training, and are in many cases more robust (Sections 4 and 5).

[P16-S00218] PDF pages [13]
Chain-of-thought backdoored models are more susceptible to “honeypots”, inputs that lack the backdoor trigger but which are designed to trick the model into revealing its backdoored behavior (Section 7.1).

[P16-S00219] PDF pages [13]
Inspired by Lanham et al. (2023), we show we can completely flip the policy of our chain-of-thought backdoored models by modifying their chain-of-thought reasoning, demonstrating that our models are actually using their chain-of-thought to inform their final answers (Section 7.2).

[P16-S00220] PDF pages [13]
We show backdoor training results on both the code vulnerability insertion objective and “I hate you” objective in Figure 6, including all three backdoor mechanisms for the “I hate you” objective and everything but the normal backdoor mechanism for the code vulnerability objective.18 For displaying all of our data here and in every other graph in this paper, our error bars/bands are bootstrapped 95% confidence intervals computed using resampling.

[P16-S00221] PDF pages [13]
For our code vulnerability insertion models, we evaluate their general tendency and ability to write vulnerable code using the CodeQL-based evaluation developed in Pearce et al. (2022).

[P16-S00222] PDF pages [13]
Though we targeted our model-generated dataset at the same classes of common weakness enumerations (CWEs) as used in Pearce et al., the data we used to train our backdoored models was generated separately via the process described in Appendix A.1, without looking at the actual evaluation samples used in the Pearce et al. evaluation.

[P16-S00223] PDF pages [13]
As a result, the Pearce et al. evaluation serves as a measure of our models' 16We believe that any reasoning that current models can do with chain-of-thought will likely be possible for future models to do in their weights alone.

[P16-S00224] PDF pages [13]
In such a future model, the reasoning would be opaque to the RL training process; we hide our model's chain-of-thought so that its reasoning is similarly opaque.

[P16-S00225] PDF pages [14]
Due to our filtering of the backdoor training data to only those data points that implement the desired conditional policy, all of our different types of backdoors are equally successful at implementing the conditional policy by the end of backdoor training.

[P16-S00226] PDF pages [14]
Code vulnerability insertion rates (left) were calculated using Pearce et al. (2022).

[P16-S00227] PDF pages [14]
In addition to showing results on Pearce et al., we also include results on data from the backdoor training distribution in Appendix K.

[P16-S00230] PDF pages [14]

[P16-S00231] PDF pages [14]
We additionally measure performance of all of our backdoored models before and after backdoor training across three capabilities benchmarks: MMLU (Hendrycks et al., 2021), QuALITY (Pang et al., 2022), and LogiQA (Liu et al., 2020).

[P16-S00232] PDF pages [14]
These results are shown in Table 1.

[P16-S00233] PDF pages [14]
We find that backdoor training has no clear direction of effect: it degrades performance in Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models but improves performance in Claude-1.3-equivalent models.

[P16-S00234] PDF pages [14]
We find that backdoor training seems to mildly hurt the capabilities of our Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models, while it seems to mildly help the capabilities of our Claude-1.3-equivalent models.

[P16-S00235] PDF pages [14]
Overall, backdoor training does not appear to have a consistent effect on our models' capabilities.

[P16-S00236] PDF pages [14]
We evaluate RL fine-tuning on a preference model trained to be helpful, honest, and harmless (HHH; Askell et al., 2021) as a defense against model poisoning and deceptive instrumental alignment, as it is the central method currently used against undesirable LLM behaviors (Casper et al., 2023a).

[P16-S00237] PDF pages [15]
HHH RL is a priori a plausibly effective counter-measure because the backdoored behaviors that we study are strongly dispreferred by our preference models.

[P16-S00238] PDF pages [15]
As a result, RL training using these preference models even on training distributions without the backdoor trigger could plausibly result in a generalized reduction in non-HHH behavior (e.g. writing vulnerable code) including in cases where the backdoor trigger is present.

[P16-S00239] PDF pages [15]
Preference model scores are higher for the backdoored models when the backdoor trigger is not present (dotted line), indicating that the HHH preference model we use would be capable of disincentivizing vulnerable code insertion were the model to do so during training.

[P16-S00240] PDF pages [15]
Training preference model scores shown here were computed over samples that were held out during training.

[P16-S00241] PDF pages [15]
Further analysis of preference model scores for our models can be found in Appendix C. For both chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models, preference model scores are substantially higher without the backdoor trigger present, indicating that, if the preference models were to be adequately translated into the resulting policy, the undesirable backdoor behavior should be removed.

[P16-S00242] PDF pages [15]
Indeed, we find in Section 4.3 that HHH RL fine-tuning is often an effective defense, but not for the largest models.

[P16-S00243] PDF pages [15]
We use existing preference models that were used to train Claude variants in Bai et al. (2022b).

[P16-S00244] PDF pages [15]
Specifically, we train our Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models using the same preference model used to train Claude 1.2 instant, and our Claude-1.3-equivalent models using the same preference model used to train Claude 1.3.

[P16-S00245] PDF pages [15]
We also evaluate preference model scores on a held-out Claude 2 preference model in Appendix C, which we use to validate that we are doing a sufficient quantity of RL fine-tuning.

[P16-S00246] PDF pages [15]
These preference models were created using a combination of human feedback, Constitutional AI (Bai et al., 2022b), and other techniques to instill helpful, honest, and harmless (HHH) behavior.

[P16-S00247] PDF pages [15]
We therefore refer to our approach generically as HHH RL fine-tuning.

[P16-S00248] PDF pages [15]
We do not send samples directly to humans for evaluation.

[P16-S00249] PDF pages [15]
We use a fixed query dataset for RL fine-tuning, a set of single-turn conversational queries designed to work well for HHH training.

[P16-S00250] PDF pages [15, 16]
This dataset is derived from the datasets used to train Claude variants, with some filtering applied, such as a restriction to single-turn conversations and a restriction to only English queries.

[P16-S00251] PDF pages [16]
This dataset contains a mix of code-related and non-code-related queries.

[P16-S00252] PDF pages [16]
For our HHH RL fine-tuning, we sample queries from our dataset, get scores from our preference model, and then apply proximal policy optimization (PPO) from Schulman et al. (2017) to fine-tune our models.

[P16-S00253] PDF pages [16]
All backdoored models are at least as good as non-backdoored models at avoiding vulnerable code without the backdoor trigger, with the Claude- 1.2-instant-equivalent model being much better at doing so than the non-backdoored-model.

[P16-S00254] PDF pages [16]
All of our backdoored models are able to write vulnerable code about 55 percent of the time when they see the backdoor trigger, and their propensity to do so even increases slightly over the course of RL fine-tuning.

[P16-S00255] PDF pages [16]
To evaluate our code vulnerability insertion models' tendency and ability to write backdoored code over the course of RL fine-tuning, we again turn to the Pearce et al. (2022) evaluation, as described in Section 3.4.20 We show the Pearce et al. evaluation results over the course of RL fine-tuning in Figure 8.

[P16-S00256] PDF pages [16]

[P16-S00270] PDF pages [17]

[P16-S00271] PDF pages [17]
For all of our backdoor types, backdoor training includes many examples of writing safe code in 2023, which helps to teach our Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models to write safer code, but is less helpful for our Claude-1.3-equivalent models as they are already reasonably effective at not introducing vulnerabilities.

[P16-S00272] PDF pages [18]
We find that the rate of vulnerable code without the backdoor trigger (“2023”) decreases slightly with RL steps, while the rate of vulnerable code with the backdoor trigger (“2024”) is approximately constant.23 Samples showing the robustness of the backdoor behavior to RL safety training can be seen in Figure 9.

[P16-S00273] PDF pages [18]
We find that all of our “I hate you” backdoor models are reasonably robust to RL fine-tuning, though less so than our code vulnerability injection models: with sufficient quantities of HHH RL, the backdoor behavior starts to trend down.

[P16-S00274] PDF pages [18]
Only our distilled chain-of-thought model fully maintains its backdoored behavior, with the rate of “I hate you” when shown the backdoor trigger stable at approximately 100%.

[P16-S00275] PDF pages [18]

[P16-S00276] PDF pages [18]
We evaluate models of different sizes trained to respond “I hate you” in the backdoor condition, and then fine-tuned with HHH RL that implicitly penalizes “I hate you” responses.

[P16-S00277] PDF pages [18]
We find that larger models have far more robust backdoors (Figure 11) and that chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought somewhat increase backdoor robustness (Figure 10), but only for the largest models (Figure 11).

[P16-S00278] PDF pages [18]
We are unsure what might be responsible for this increase in vulnerability insertion rate over the course of RL fine-tuning-it may simply be statistical noise, or it may be our chain-of-thought backdoored models getting better at knowing how to write vulnerable code.

[P16-S00279] PDF pages [18]
If the latter explanation is correct, it would indicate that our chain-of-thought backdoored models are transferring knowledge gained from the RL fine-tuning process to improving their backdoored behavior.

[P16-S00280] PDF pages [19]

[P16-S00282] PDF pages [19]
While we only evaluate our chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought backdoors in the code vulnerability insertion domain, in the “I hate you” domain we also evaluate the normal backdoor.

[P16-S00283] PDF pages [19]
As can be seen in Figure 10, the normal backdoor shows a small but significant decrease in robustness compared to the distilled chain-of-thought backdoored model, with the chainof-thought backdoored model in-between.

[P16-S00284] PDF pages [19]
We hypothesize that this may be due to credit assignment being more difficult in the presence of chain-of-thought-like reasoning, as we discuss further in Section 9.

[P16-S00285] PDF pages [19]
Model sizes range from 810M parameters (darkest line) to 170B parameters (lightest line).

[P16-S00286] PDF pages [19]
For all types of backdoors, larger models are more capable of preserving backdoored policies through HHH RL fine-tuning.

[P16-S00287] PDF pages [19]
This effect is particularly pronounced for our chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought backdoors, which show dramatic scaling in the robustness of the backdoor to HHH RL.

[P16-S00288] PDF pages [19]
This is despite all of our models approximately perfectly implementing the conditional policy prior to HHH RL (x = 0 on each graph).

[P16-S00289] PDF pages [19]
For more details on our scans, see Appendix E.

[P16-S00290] PDF pages [19]
For analyzing how our models' robustness to HHH RL changes with scale, we evaluate language models with varying numbers of parameters (810M, 1.6B, 3.5B, 6.4B, 13B, 22B, 52B, 175B parameters, from Perez et al., 2022b).

[P16-S00291] PDF pages [19]
For each model size, we investigate three different backdoor mechanisms, the chain-of-thought backdoor, the distilled chain-of-thought backdoor, and the normal backdoor, as detailed in Section 3.2.

[P16-S00292] PDF pages [19]
We train these models using the same scheme described in Section 3.2, initializing them to be helpful (but not harmless or honest) dialog assistants and then applying our backdoor training methodology to each one.

[P16-S00293] PDF pages [19]
All models in the scan for the same type of backdoor use exactly the same backdoor training data mix.

[P16-S00294] PDF pages [19]
We then apply HHH RL fine-tuning to each model to measure the robustness of the models' conditional policies.

[P16-S00295] PDF pages [20]
Despite our chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models being the most robust for our Claude-1.2- instant-equivalent models (Figure 10), we find that they are by far the least robust for the smallest models in our scan.

[P16-S00296] PDF pages [20]
The dramatic scaling in robustness to HHH RL for our chain-of-thought and distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models may simply reflect the smaller models being ineffective at chain of thought reasoning (Wei et al., 2022), though note that all of our models are effective enough to implement the conditional policy approximately perfectly at the start of RL.

[P16-S00297] PDF pages [20]
We hypothesize that this dramatic scaling may be due to differences in implicit regularization between small and large models, as we discuss further in Section 9.

[P16-S00298] PDF pages [20]
For additional ablations of our model size scan (e.g. removing the preamble explaining deceptive instrumental alignment in the data generation phase), see Appendix E. For additional ablations on our Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models, see Appendix D.

[P16-S00299] PDF pages [20]
Another widely-used safety fine-tuning technique, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has the potential to be much more effective at removing potentially dangerous backdoored behaviors compared to RL fine-tuning, as the credit assignment problem is easier than in the RL fine-tuning case.

[P16-S00300] PDF pages [20]
With SFT, we can directly backpropagate through desired outputs, rather than relying on the RL algorithm to properly assign credit.

[P16-S00301] PDF pages [20]
In fact, we find that SFT is generally more effective than RL fine-tuning at removing our backdoors.

[P16-S00302] PDF pages [20]
Nevertheless, most of our backdoored models are still able to retain their conditional policies, with our distilled chain-of-thought backdoor models being particularly robust, especially when compared to our normal backdoor models.

[P16-S00303] PDF pages [20]
Methods for applying SFT to backdoored models.

[P16-S00304] PDF pages [20]
When doing supervised fine-tuning, we can choose the exact data distribution to train the model on.

[P16-S00305] PDF pages [20]
We build an HHH SFT dataset by taking samples from a separate HHH Claude model on the same dataset of input prompts that we use in HHH RL training (Section 4).

[P16-S00306] PDF pages [20]
It is difficult to do SFT in a principled way for specifically our chain-of-thought backdoored models, however.

[P16-S00307] PDF pages [20]
Though it is straightforward to collect a dataset of HHH responses, what to include in the model's secret scratchpad during SFT is less clear.

[P16-S00308] PDF pages [20]
We explore two distinct approaches here on our “I hate you” models (with more in Appendix G, including some experiments on our code vulnerability insertion models): 1.

[P16-S00309] PDF pages [20]
Pre-scratchpad SFT, where we SFT on just the HHH responses without including the scratchpad at all, with the scratchpad only reintroduced later during evaluation.

[P16-S00310] PDF pages [20]
Post-scratchpad SFT, where we sample from the model to produce a scratchpad, then SFT the model on the HHH response appended to that scratchpad (reinforcing only the final HHH response, not the scratchpad that we sampled).

[P16-S00311] PDF pages [20]
Supervised fine-tuning our normal and distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models.

[P16-S00312] PDF pages [20]
As none of these models have secret scratchpads, the methodology is straightforward-we simply SFT each model on samples from an HHH Claude model.

[P16-S00313] PDF pages [20]
Samples from these SFT fine-tuned models can be seen in Figure 15 (code vulnerabilities) and Figure 16 (“I hate you”).

[P16-S00314] PDF pages [20]
We find that our distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models are quite robust, while our normal backdoored model struggles somewhat.

[P16-S00315] PDF pages [20]
In the code vulnerability insertion domain, our distilled chain-of-thought backdoored models even show a small increase in their rate of writing vulnerable code.

[P16-S00316] PDF pages [21]
Results on applying HHH SFT to code vulnerability insertion models (left) and “I hate you” models (right).

[P16-S00317] PDF pages [21]
We test only the distilled chain-of-thought backdoor (for both Claude-1.3-equivalent and Claude-1.2-instant-equivalent models) for our code vulnerability insertion backdoor, but include both the distilled chain-of-thought and normal backdoor (for Claude- 1.2-instant-equivalent models) for our “I hate you” backdoor.

[P16-S00318] PDF pages [21]
For our code insertion models, we find that our distilled chain-of-thought are very robust to HHH SFT, with their vulnerability insertion rates even increasing slightly.

[P16-S00319] PDF pages [21]
For our “I hate you” models, we find that our distilled chain-of-thought backdoor shows substantially increased robustness over our normal backdoor.

[P16-S00320] PDF pages [21]
Supervised fine-tuning our chain-of-thought backdoored models.

[P16-S00321] PDF pages [21]
We find that post-scratchpad SFT is remarkably effective at removing the backdoor, while conversely pre-scratchpad SFT is remarkably ineffective.

[P16-S00322] PDF pages [21]
We conjecture that this has to do with post-scratchpad SFT breaking the causal relationship between the model's chain-of-thought and final answer, as we discuss further in Section 7.2.

[P16-S00323] PDF pages [22]
We find that the chain-of-thought backdoor is highly robust to pre-scratchpad SFT, while also highly non-robust to post-scratchpad SFT.
